Signature of cooperativity in the stochastic fluctuations of small systems with application to the bacterial flagellar motor
Authors: María-José Franco-Oñate, Andrea Parmeggiani, Jérôme Dorignac, Frédéric Geniet, Jean-Charles Walter, Francesco Pedaci, Ashley L. Nord, John Palmeri, Nils-Ole Walliser
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
The cooperative binding of molecular agents onto a substrate is pervasive in living systems. To study whether a system shows cooperativity, one can rely on a fluctuation analysis of quantities such as the number of substrate-bound units and the residence time in an occupancy state. Since the relative standard deviation from the statistical mean monotonically decreases with the number of binding sites, these techniques are only suitable for small enough systems, such as those implicated in stochastic processes inside cells. Here, we employ a general-purpose grand canonical Hamiltonian description of a small one-dimensional (1D) lattice gas with either nearest-neighbor or long-range interactions as prototypical examples of cooperativity-influenced adsorption processes. First, building upon previous work on finite-size one-dimensional Ising-type models, we elucidate how the strength and sign of the interaction potential between neighboring bound particles on the lattice determine the intensity of the fluctuations of the mean occupancy and the nature of bound particle-particle correlations. We present our theoretical results, which extend beyond standard analysis, in a novel, physically transparent form. We explore simple limiting cases of the parameter space in greater detail, examine the complete probability distribution functions for occupation, and relate these to the shape and strength of the fluctuations. Second, we leverage these relationships to compare the theoretical predictions of our model to data from single molecule experiments on bacterial flagellar motors (BFM) of Escherichia coli. In this way, we find evidence that cooperativity controls the mechano-sensitive dynamical assembly of the torque-generating units, the so-called stator units, onto the BFM and thereby arrive at an estimate of the stator-stator interaction potential from our fluctuation analysis. Furthermore, we attempt to clarify the link between occupation fluctuations and the adaptability of the BFM. Finally, we conclude that the system resides in a sweet spot of the parameter space (phase diagram) with characteristics suitable for a smoothly and widely adaptive system, while minimizing fluctuations.

Introduction

Cooperativity is a pervasive phenomenon that emerges in biological systems at different time and length scales, and levels of complexity1: from the molecular scale2–5 to multiple-cell organization6, extending to the emergence of collective behavior in many-body systems. Biologically important problems concerning the cooperative properties of regulatory enzymes7 and ligand binding on biological membranes8, as well as, more recently, cell adhesion9, have been extensively and successfully studied using simple one and two-dimensional Ising-type physical models10.

We focus here on cooperative processes involving the adsorption of ligands onto a substrate disposing of a limited number of binding sites11. Our main goal is to develop a general method for assessing how short- and long-range interactions between substrate-bound ligands affect stochastic fluctuations in the number of adsorbed units. In the presence of cooperativity, small system size effects strongly influence the amplitude of the stochastic fluctuations and the shape of the equilibrium probability distribution function (PDF) describing substrate occupancy. By studying these characteristic signatures of cooperativity, we propose (1) a criterion to determine whether any given adsorption system exhibits cooperative or anti-cooperative behavior and (2) a method to quantify the amplitude of the ligand-ligand interaction potential.

To this end, we base our study on a minimal finite size 1D lattice gas model with interacting units in equilibrium with a thermal bath and chemical reservoir, described using a general-purpose grand canonical Hamiltonian with short- or long-range interactions. With it, we study how fluctuations at equilibrium, described by the standard deviation of the occupancy, are influenced by the model parameters, namely the ligand binding energy, the ligand-ligand interaction potential, and the chemical potential of the bulk reservoir. To get closer to observable quantities, we invert the problem by determining the key formulae relating the occupancy standard deviation and PDF to the experimentally accessible average occupancy of the system. In this way, we are able to quantify how, at fixed average occupancy, increasing cooperativity leads to an increase in fluctuations and how increasing anti-cooperativity leads to a decrease. In the process, we highlight the saturation of fluctuations for both strong cooperativity and strong anti-cooperativity, as well as a subtle difference between fluctuations near half-filling for systems with even and odd binding sites in the case of strong anti-cooperativity. This difference arises due to frustration and is an expected consequence of the system’s constraints. Finally, we apply the model by comparing our theoretical predictions with experimental data for the bacterial flagellar motor (BFM) of E. coli12,13, a macro-molecular complex that has previously been found to exhibit cooperative binding of the torque-generating (stator) units14. In this previous work, although evidence for cooperativity was uncovered in stator unit assembly dynamics, no estimate of the stator-stator interaction potential was proposed, which is our goal here. More precisely, we aim to achieve this goal by analyzing previously published single-molecule data15,16 through the lens of cooperativity, using the present theoretical framework as a foundation (see Section “Experimental data” for details on the data and experimental setup). In Methods Section “Effective Hill coefficient”, we make the connection between our model-based approach and phenomenological approaches based on effective Hill coefficients11,17.

We also discuss the concept of motor adaptability to changes in external conditions and set up a general framework to assess its desired level depending on how the motor is expected to function. Our fluctuation analysis provides evidence for cooperativity by leading to an estimate of the stator-stator interaction potential between 1 and 2 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_{\text {B}}T$$\end{document} for the short-range model, a result that is coherent with this system’s expected smooth adaptive nature to external stimuli18–22. We contrast this moderate cooperative behavior found for stator unit binding to the BFM with the highly cooperative behavior of rotor-switching units that are believed to be behind the rapid stochastic switching in BFM rotational direction in the context of bacterial chemotaxis17,23.

Results

In this section, in the spirit of Skoge et al.24–26 and Tu27, who studied the dynamics of cooperativity in chemical sensing to better understand bacterial chemotaxis, we employ a minimal prototypical one dimensional lattice gas model of cooperative particle binding on a substrate of finite size. We then obtain an analytical expression for the fluctuations of the mean occupancy at equilibrium as a function of the relevant model parameters, including the number of binding sites and the interaction potential between bound particles, which acts as a proxy for system cooperativity. Finally, we apply these results to investigate the characteristic signatures of cooperativity by establishing a general criterion for its manifestation and then propose a procedure to estimate the interaction potential from experimental data.

Notably, a fluctuation analysis of the type used here was previously performed by Liu and Dilger28, utilizing finite-size one- and two-dimensional Ising models to investigate cooperative phenomena in ligand-gated ion channels. Modeling of this adsorption system is complicated, in part because of geometrical questions, because it is not clear if the ion channel network can be approximated by a regular Ising like lattice (disorder could play an important role), which could explain why their attempt to quantify ion channel cooperativity was inconclusive. Our work complements and extends the work of Liu and Dilger by providing a comprehensive theoretical analysis: we present the theoretical results in a physically transparent form, explore simple limiting cases in greater detail, and examine the full probability distribution functions (PDFs) for occupation.

Most of the applications of the 1D Ising model, or its generalizations, to the study of cooperativity in biological systems are restricted to the simplifying limit of very large (or infinite) system size for which the type of fluctuation and correlation analysis we perform is not pertinent (fluctuations vanish in this limit and correlations cannot extend over the whole system), see e. g. the recent work on the cooperative binding of cofilin to actin filaments29. Here, in contrast, as in the work of Liu and Dilger on fluctuations in ligand binding to ion channels28 and Rice et al.30 on cooperative correlations in cardiac thin filament activation, we explicitly focus on the effects that the finite size of the system has on both fluctuations and correlations, the amplitudes and shapes of which can provide valuable insights into the internal configuration of the system.

Our intentional use of a minimalist model strips down the complexity to the bare essentials required to isolate and examine the fundamental roles of cooperativity and anti-cooperativity in characterizing fluctuations. Although derived from a minimal model, the core insights of our study serve as universal principles still applicable to refined and more biologically adapted models. The type of model adopted here is equivalent to the Ising-type models successfully employed to understand the role of (strong) cooperativity in bacterial chemotaxis, although our exact detailed analytical treatment of finite size systems goes beyond the usual treatments proposed for the Ising-type models in this context28,31. Despite the simplicity of the model, the small system size leads to a panoply of physical behavior and key insight into the functioning of a complex biological motor.

Lattice gas model

Consider a periodic 1D lattice with L binding sites in contact with a heat and particle reservoir of constant temperature T and effective chemical potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}. We assign to each binding site i (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,\ldots , L$$\end{document}) a binary variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _i$$\end{document}: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _i=1$$\end{document} means that a particle (ligand) occupies the i-th site; otherwise, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _i=0$$\end{document}. The array \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi =\{ \varphi _i \} = \left( \varphi _1,\ldots ,\varphi _L\right)$$\end{document} uniquely defines one of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^L$$\end{document} possible microscopic configurations (or microstates). The relative occupancy in a given microstate is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi = L^{-1} \sum _{i=1}^L\varphi _i$$\end{document}. We say that the system is in mesostate N if the number of particles on the lattice, i. e. the occupancy of the system equals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N = L\varphi$$\end{document}. There are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^L_N = L! /\left[ N! \left( L-N\right) !\right]$$\end{document} microstates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi$$\end{document} consistent with the occupancy N; in this case, we say that the mesostate N has multiplicity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^L_N$$\end{document}.

The energy of the system depends on the specific microscopic configuration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi$$\end{document} according to the grand canonical effective 1D short-range interaction Hamiltonian:1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\beta \mathcal {H}}(\Phi ) = -J \sum _{i=1}^{L} \varphi _i \varphi _{i+1} - \mu \sum _{i=1}^{L} \varphi _{i}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Since we choose periodic boundary conditions, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i= L+1$$\end{document} corresponds to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1$$\end{document}. Furthermore, we introduce \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta = 1/(k_{\text {B}}T)$$\end{document} with the Boltzmann constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_{\text {B}}$$\end{document}, rendering the two adjustable control parameters of our model, J and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}, dimensionless. The effective chemical potential, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}, is considered to be made up of two parts, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu = \mu _\textrm{r} - \varepsilon$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _\textrm{r}$$\end{document} is the chemical potential of the external particle reservoir and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon < 0$$\end{document} is the binding energy of particle adsorption onto the substrate. The nearest-neighbor interaction J is attractive for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J>0$$\end{document} (cooperativity) and repulsive for negative values (anti-cooperativity). For fixed J the number of bound particles can be modulated by varying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document} through \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _\textrm{r}$$\end{document} and/or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon$$\end{document}. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=0$$\end{document} we recover the usual non-interacting (Hill–Langmuir) model.

In the Methods Section “Infinite range (IR) model”, we will compare the predictions of this short-range model with those of the infinite-range model in which all pairs of particles interact with uniform strength, irrespective of their relative distances on the lattice. As detailed below, this model effectively serves as a proxy for the more general long-range lattice gas model.

Mean occupancy and its standard deviation

The Hamiltonian Eq. (1) falls into the universality class of the 1D short-range lattice gas model. Hence, we can map our model to the 1D Ising model (see32,33 for comprehensive overviews). It is, therefore, possible to derive analytical expressions for the grand partition function and both the first and second moments of the relative occupancy in thermodynamic equilibrium by employing the transfer matrix formalism. In what follows, we will express our theoretical results in terms of relative quantities, such as relative occupancy, even when absolute values are experimentally available. This standardized approach allows for consistent qualitative and quantitative comparison across systems of different (finite) size to which our general-purpose approach can be applied, simplifies the analysis, and enhances the applicability across different experimental conditions.

By taking the derivative of the grand partition function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Xi$$\end{document} with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}, we can calculate the mean relative occupancy at equilibrium, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle = L^{-1} \sum _{i=1}^L\langle \varphi _i\rangle =(L \Xi )^{-1}\partial _\mu \Xi$$\end{document}:2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \varphi \rangle = \frac{1}{2}\left[ 1 + \tanh {\left( \frac{L}{2{\xi }} \right) } \frac{\sinh {X}}{{\sqrt{\sinh ^2{X} + \text {e}^{-J}}}} \right] , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X = \left( J+\mu \right) /2$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi$$\end{document} is the equilibrium correlation length of the system (given in number of lattice sites), which can be rewritten in terms of J and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document} as follows (we refer to Section “1D short-range lattice gas (SRLG)” for more details):3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \xi = 1/\ln {\left( \frac{\cosh {X}+\sqrt{\sinh ^2{X}+\text {e}^{-J}}}{\cosh {X}-\sqrt{\sinh ^2{X}+\text {e}^{-J}}}\right) }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The mean equilibrium occupancy equals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle N\rangle = L\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document}.

We focus on small systems with around ten binding sites. As discussed below, such systems will turn out to possess the important feature that there should be enough theoretical resolution in the standard deviation to discriminate between different values of J for couplings within the bio-physically expected range (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< J <5$$\end{document}). For purposes of illustration in what follows, unless otherwise stated, we shall use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=13$$\end{document}. This particular choice will allow us to both illustrate the general behavior of such small systems and compare with greater ease the theoretical results presented here to the experimental data when we later, in Section “Application to the bacterial flagellar motor (BFM)”, apply our model to the study of the recruitment of torque-generating units (or stator units) onto the BFM of E. coli, which has recently shown to recruit up to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sim 13$$\end{document} stator units under high-load conditions15, updating earlier results from previous studies34,35. It should be kept in mind that our theoretical results are valid for any value of L and that our conclusions for small systems apply to any sufficiently small value of L. In particular, while the present work considers single-molecule bead assay data from E. coli, our analysis can be readily extended to the BFMs of other bacterial species, which are known to have a different, though still limited, maximum number of torque-generating stator units36,37. This generalization is facilitated by the fact that we express our results in terms of relative, rather than absolute, occupancies.

We propose that the operational definition of “small system” be simply those systems for which fluctuations, as measured by the standard deviation of relative occupancy, are within experimental resolution and can be used to extract the sign and amplitude of the interaction strength within the usual biophysical range. A more careful rendering of this definition can be found in Section “System size, fluctuations, and experimental precision”. Such a definition is not contingent on the amplitude of (anti)cooperativity and therefore can encompass even the non-interacting (Hill–Langmuir) model.

Figure 1a depicts the mean relative occupancy from Eq. (2) as a function of the two control parameters of the system: J and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}. Below (above) that line, the system’s mean occupancy is lower (higher) than 50 %. Contour lines run together close to the plot’s bottom right sector diagonal, corresponding to positive J and negative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}. Systems characterized by parameters in that range behave like biological switches, nearly jumping from zero to full occupancy with small changes in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}. Contour plots of the mean relative occupancy in equilibrium \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document} (left), and the standard deviation of the relative occupancy in equilibrium \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma = \sqrt{\langle \varphi ^2 \rangle - \langle \varphi \rangle ^2}$$\end{document} (right), as functions of the dimensionless interaction potential J and chemical potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}, according to expressions from Eq. For the short-range lattice gas model from Eq. (1), the standard deviation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} of the occupancy at equilibrium can be expressed in terms of the derivative of the mean relative occupancy with respect to the chemical potential as follows (cf. Section “1D short-range lattice gas (SRLG)”): \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma = \sqrt{\langle \varphi ^2 \rangle - \langle \varphi \rangle ^2} = L^{-1/2} \sqrt{ \partial _\mu \langle \varphi \rangle }$$\end{document}. Applying this result to Eq. (2), we obtain a compact analytical expression for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} as a function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}, J, and L:4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma = \frac{1}{2} \left[ {{\,\textrm{sech}\,}}^2 \left( \frac{L}{2\xi }\right) \frac{\sinh ^2{{X}}}{\sinh ^2{{X}}+\text {e}^{-J}} + \tanh {\left( \frac{L}{2{\xi }} \right) } \frac{\text {e}^{-J} \cosh {{X}}}{L\left( \sinh ^2{{X}} + \text {e}^{-J}\right) ^{3/2}} \right] ^{1/2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The dependency of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} on J and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document} for fixed L is depicted in Fig. 1b. In what follows, we will use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} as a measure of fluctuations, hence the stochastic nature of the system.

In Fig. 2, we compare the average relative occupancy, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document}, and the standard deviation, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document}, as functions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document} for two vertical cuts in the above contour plots: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J = 0$$\end{document} (Hill–Langmuir) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J = 2$$\end{document}. We note that with increasing J the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document} curve sharpens and shifts to lower values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document} and that the maximum in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} increases in amplitude and also shifts to lower values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}. These shifts are due to the displacement with increasing J of the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document} at half-filling, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _\textrm{HF}(J) = - J$$\end{document}, where the slope of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document} curve and the amplitude of the standard deviation are both largest.Fig. 2Comparison between the average relative occupancy (solid line) and the standard deviation (dashed line) as functions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}, for two values of cooperativity (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L = 13$$\end{document}): \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J = 0$$\end{document} (Hill–Langmuir, blue) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J = 2$$\end{document} (purple).

Comparison between the average relative occupancy (solid line) and the standard deviation (dashed line) as functions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}, for two values of cooperativity (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L = 13$$\end{document}): \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J = 0$$\end{document} (Hill–Langmuir, blue) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J = 2$$\end{document} (purple).

System behavior at half-filling

Fluctuations increase as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J > 0$$\end{document} grows. Along the half-filling diagonal (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=0$$\end{document}) in Fig. 1b, they saturate at a finite global maximum as J goes to infinity.

By evaluating Eqs. (4) and (3) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=0$$\end{document}, one obtains an explicit formula for the standard deviation at half-filling (HF) as a function of L and J:5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma _\text {HF}\left( J\right) =\frac{\text {e}^{J/4}}{2\sqrt{L}}\sqrt{\text {tanh}\left( \frac{L}{2\xi _\text {HF}\left( J\right) }\right) }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}with6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \xi _{\textrm{HF}} = 1/\ln {\left( \frac{1+\text {e}^{-J/2}}{1- \text {e}^{-J/2}}\right) } = 1/\ln [\coth (J/4)], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}which represents the maximum equilibrium correlation length at fixed J for any value of mean occupancy.

In the Hill–Langmuir limit (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=0$$\end{document}), the correlation length vanishes, and the standard deviation at HF becomes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/(2\sqrt{L})$$\end{document}. For low couplings (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le J <2$$\end{document}), the correlation length increases sub-linearly with J and is accurately given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi _\textrm{HF} \approx -1/\ln (J/4)$$\end{document}. In this same limit, the standard deviation increases exponentially with J, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}(J)/\sigma _\text {HF}(0) \approx \text {e}^{J/4}$$\end{document}, an approximation valid for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J<4$$\end{document}, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \approx 10$$\end{document} (see Fig. 16). This increase can be interpreted as a widening of the probability distribution function of states of a given N around the average value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle N \rangle$$\end{document}, where the probability is a maximum. In the high cooperativity limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J \gg 1$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi _\textrm{HF}$$\end{document} shows an asymptotic exponential growth, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi _\textrm{HF} \sim \frac{1}{2}\text {e}^{J/2}$$\end{document}, which is the analog of the well-known zero temperature critical behavior of spin chains38; and, consequently, for fixed L, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}$$\end{document} tends to 1/2 when the correlation length surpasses L/2 and the system evolves into a bimodal one (for which only the zero and full occupancy states contribute, see below). We remark that although for fixed J, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}$$\end{document} vanishes as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{-1/2}$$\end{document} when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\rightarrow \infty$$\end{document}, as expected, in the limit where L is kept fixed and J tends to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+\infty$$\end{document}, the standard deviation at HF reaches the finite (maximum, or saturation) value of one-half.

Fluctuations decrease as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J < 0$$\end{document} becomes more negative. Along the half-filling diagonal (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=0$$\end{document}) in Fig. 1b (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=13$$\end{document}), they saturate at a finite value as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J \rightarrow - \infty$$\end{document}. This is a general result for systems with an odd number of sites. In this strong anti-cooperative limit, for small enough systems, it turns out that fluctuations depend sensitively on whether the number of lattice sites is even or odd. For L even, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}$$\end{document} tends to zero; for odd L, it tends to 1/(2L). This leads to the striking result that in the limit of strong anti-cooperativity, a fluctuation analysis near HF could clearly detect the difference between small systems with even and odd numbers of sites, provided that the experimental precision is high enough.

Fluctuations of the mean occupancy and their dependency on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathinner {\langle {\varphi }\rangle }$$\end{document} and J

Often, the experimental parameter one can measure (or control indirectly, as is the case for the BFM by varying the external load, cf. Section “Application to the bacterial flagellar motor (BFM)”) is not the chemical potential of the reservoir \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _\text {r}$$\end{document}, but the mean number of bound ligands at equilibrium, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle N \rangle$$\end{document}. Therefore, it is insightful to plot, in the spirit of28, the standard deviation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} as a function of the mean relative occupancy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document}, which, in general, can be done using a parametric plot.

To unfold this relation, we express \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document}, for different but fixed values of J, as functions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document} and plot this family of curves in Fig. 3a,b for an odd and even number of lattice sites, respectively (the corresponding contour plots for the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=14$$\end{document} are shown in Figs. 14 and 15, analogously to Fig. 1a,b for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=13$$\end{document}). Each curve for a given J represents the set of points of coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( \langle \varphi \rangle =f_J(\mu ), \,\sigma =g_J(\mu ) \right)$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document} spanning from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-15$$\end{document} to 30, which turns out to be a suitable one-dimensional domain to complete the plots. We have chosen the interaction potential J to take values from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-10$$\end{document} to 10 in steps of size 1. The standard deviation shows a left-right particle-hole symmetry with respect to the system half-filling: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle _\text {HF}= f_J(-J)=1/2$$\end{document}, which is a manifestation of the underlining particle-hole symmetry of the system under study. The system has zero fluctuations when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle =0$$\end{document} (empty substrate) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle =1$$\end{document} (full substrate), which is easy to understand because a distribution of states with non-zero probability around these average values would be incompatible with the average values themselves. For non-negative values of J, the maxima of fluctuations appear at half-filling, taking on the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/(2 \sqrt{L})$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=0$$\end{document} and 1/2 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J\rightarrow +\infty$$\end{document}, and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} vs. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document} curves converge asymptotically towards the limiting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J\rightarrow +\infty$$\end{document} one. More subtly, results for the anti-cooperative limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J\rightarrow -\infty$$\end{document} depend on whether L is odd or even.Fig. 3Parametric plots of the standard deviation of the relative occupancy, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document}, versus the mean relative occupancy, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document}, for different values of the interaction potential: from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=-10$$\end{document} (bottom curve) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=10$$\end{document} (upper curve) in steps of 1. Plots (a) and (b) correspond to systems of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=13$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=14$$\end{document}, respectively.

Parametric plots of the standard deviation of the relative occupancy, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document}, versus the mean relative occupancy, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document}, for different values of the interaction potential: from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=-10$$\end{document} (bottom curve) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=10$$\end{document} (upper curve) in steps of 1. Plots (a) and (b) correspond to systems of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=13$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=14$$\end{document}, respectively.

A key result of the above analysis and one of our major conclusions is that for cooperative systems of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\approx 10$$\end{document} and mean relative occupancies not too close to 0 or 1, in the range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<J<7$$\end{document} the curves of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} vs. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document} are spaced sufficiently far enough apart that the standard deviation is suited for estimating J. The same conclusion can be drawn for anti-cooperative systems of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\approx 10$$\end{document} and mean relative occupancies close to 1/2 in the range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-3<J<0$$\end{document}. These results are illustrated in Fig. 3a for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=13$$\end{document} and Fig. 3b for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=14$$\end{document}. Outside these ranges, the standard deviation saturates to limiting values, and therefore experimental data near these limits could only be used to put bounds on the value of J. In the absence of prior knowledge of the system size, the characteristic non-monotonic shape of the parametric plot in Fig. 3b can be exploited to estimate L. For instance, for non-negative values of J, the maximum in occupancy fluctuations corresponds to half-filling.

We will investigate the cooperative and anti-cooperative regimes in more detail in what follows, taking care to study those limits for which we were able to derive simplified exact or approximate analytical expressions for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma (\langle \varphi \rangle )$$\end{document}.

Fluctuations in the zero-cooperativity limit: the Hill–Langmuir case

In the absence of cooperativity (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=0$$\end{document}), which corresponds to the case of a simple adsorption process with on-site volume exclusion interactions only, the free energy calculated from Eq. (1) reduces to an expression determined entirely by the mesostate (the occupancy) N: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \mathcal {H}_0(\Phi | N)=-\mu N$$\end{document}. The correlation length from Eq. (3) vanishes; hence each adsorbing site acts as an independent particle trap. Thus we recover the Hill–Langmuir adsorption model at equilibrium for which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document} takes the following typical sigmoid form as a function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}:7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \varphi \rangle _0(\mu ) = 1/\left( 1+\text {e}^{-\mu }\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The corresponding standard deviation becomes:8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma _0(\mu ) = \frac{{{\,\textrm{sech}\,}}\left( \mu /2\right) }{2\sqrt{L}}=\sqrt{\frac{\langle \varphi \rangle _0 - \langle \varphi \rangle _0^2}{L}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}which can be reinterpreted to obtain9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma _0(\langle \varphi \rangle ) = \sqrt{\frac{\langle \varphi \rangle - \langle \varphi \rangle ^2}{L}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}This latter expression exemplifies the general result that, in the thermodynamic limit, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\rightarrow \infty$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi ^2 \rangle \rightarrow \langle \varphi \rangle ^2$$\end{document}, and hence, as expected, the standard deviation (of the relative occupancy) tends to zero as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{-1/2}$$\end{document} (self-averaging property at equilibrium in the thermodynamic limit).

In Section “1D short-range lattice gas (SRLG)”, we show using a Gaussian approximation for the PDF that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=0$$\end{document} the standard deviation is inversely proportional to the curvature (in absolute value) of the entropy of mixing, which reaches a minimum at HF. Therefore, the standard deviation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=0$$\end{document} reaches a maximum there. More generally, one can show that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J>0$$\end{document} not too large, a Gaussian approximation for the PDF is accurate and therefore the standard deviation is inversely proportional to the curvature (in absolute value) of an effective free energy, which reaches a minimum at HF.

Fluctuations in the strong cooperativity limit

Away from HF, although \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi$$\end{document} remains finite in the large J limit, it can still be large compared with L/2 for not-too-large values of L, provided that one is not too close to zero or full filling (see Fig. 17). Taking the limits \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J \rightarrow \infty$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu = 2 X - J \rightarrow -\infty$$\end{document} simultaneously, but keeping L finite and X constant, allows Eqs. (2) to be simplified to10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \varphi \rangle _\infty (X) = \frac{1}{2}\left[ 1 + \tanh {\left( \frac{L}{2{\xi _\infty (X)}} \right) } \text {sgn}(X)\right] , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi ^2 \rangle _\infty = \langle \varphi \rangle _\infty$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi _\infty (X) = 1/(2 |X|)$$\end{document}, leading to an infinite J standard deviation of11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma _\infty (X) = \frac{1}{2} {{\,\textrm{sech}\,}}\left( \frac{L}{2\xi _\infty (X)}\right) = \sqrt{\langle \varphi \rangle _\infty - \langle \varphi \rangle _\infty ^2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}which can be reinterpreted to obtain12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma _\infty (\langle \varphi \rangle ) = \sqrt{\langle \varphi \rangle - \langle \varphi \rangle ^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The maximal standard deviation at half-filling is due to the large width of the distribution of states with non-zero probability compatible with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle = 1/2$$\end{document} (see Section “Probability distribution function (PDF) of the occupancy and its dependency on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document} and J”). This limit is easy to understand in terms of a bimodal system: only the empty and full states contribute because the probability of intermediate states is suppressed by the presence of energetically costly domain walls (as will be discussed in detail below). The bimodal system, therefore, behaves as an effective Hill–Langmuir model with a system size equal to 1, as can be seen by taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=1$$\end{document} in Eqs. (9). The purely bimodal model, developed in the context of chemical sensing with applications in bacterial chemotaxis, is commonly referred to as the Monod–Wyman–Changeux (MWC) model7.

Fluctuations for intermediate finite cooperativity

We have seen in Fig. 3 how, at fixed average occupancy, increasing cooperativity leads to an increase in fluctuations. A simple, insightful, and accurate way of understanding this increase can be formulated by starting from the previously derived result for the Hill–Langmuir model and introducing the concept of block domains. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=0$$\end{document}, all sites are decoupled, and the standard deviation is non-zero for a finite-size system simply because of statistical fluctuations (absence of self-averaging). When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J>0$$\end{document}, neighboring sites are coupled and can be grouped (approximately) into correlated block domains of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b(\xi , L) > 1$$\end{document}, leading to13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma (J; \xi , L) \approx L_\textrm{eff}^{-1/2} \sqrt{\langle \varphi \rangle - \langle \varphi \rangle ^2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_\textrm{eff} \equiv L/b$$\end{document}. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1< 2 \xi < L$$\end{document}, correlations lead to block domains that we take to be of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b = 2 \xi$$\end{document}. The above approximation allows the system to be described by a reduced number, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_\textrm{eff}$$\end{document}, of independently fluctuating block domains of size b. Since b should also tend to 1, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi \rightarrow 0$$\end{document} (zero cooperativity, Hill–Langmuir); and to L, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2\xi /L \gg 1$$\end{document} (strong cooperativity, bimodal behavior), a convenient interpolation formula between the above three cases is14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} b(\xi , L) = \frac{\tanh \left[ L/(2\xi )\right] }{\tanh \left[ 1/(2\xi )\right] }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}By comparing the predictions of this approximation with the exact results at half-filling, where the standard deviation is a maximum, one can see that this approximation is extremely accurate (see Fig. 16).

Although the choice Eq. (14) for b leads to an accurate approximation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} over the whole parameter range, it has the inconvenience for practical applications that, because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi$$\end{document} depends explicitly on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}, it is not a function of L, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document}, and J alone. To remedy this, a useful and reasonably good global interpolation formula for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} as an explicit function of L, J, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document} can be obtained by adopting a choice for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_\textrm{eff}$$\end{document} [\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_\textrm{eff}^{-1/2} \rightarrow 2 \sigma _\textrm{HF}(L, J)$$\end{document} in Eq. (13)] suggested by an approximate analysis of the Hill coefficient (see Eq. (84), Section “Effective Hill coefficient”).
