\langle \varphi \rangle ) \approx \frac{\exp \left[ -\frac{(\varphi - \langle \varphi \rangle )^2}{2 (\sigma _d^\textrm{even})^2} \right] }{{(2 \pi )^{1/2} \sigma _d^\textrm{even} L}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where we have used67\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Xi _d^{\textrm{even}}(\langle \varphi \rangle ) = \frac{2}{( 1-2\langle \varphi \rangle )^{L/2}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}and68\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _d^{\textrm{even}}(\langle \varphi \rangle ) = \ln \left( \frac{2 \langle \varphi \rangle }{1-2 \langle \varphi \rangle } \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The Gaussian PDF approximation is very accurate compared with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_d^\textrm{even}(N; \langle \varphi \rangle )$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=14$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle = 1/3$$\end{document}, although \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_d^\textrm{even}(N; \langle \varphi \rangle )$$\end{document} itself underestimates the exact maximum by about 30% and cannot capture the skewness observed in the exact results (see Fig. 5). The particle-hole pair approximation does reproduce accurately, however, the width of the exact PDF.

In the strong anti-cooperativity limit (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J \rightarrow - \infty$$\end{document}) for L odd, the system cannot be approximated by non-overlapping particle-hole pairs at HF. We need to account for a defect in order to approach HF. The restricted grand partition function for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \langle \varphi \rangle \le 1/2$$\end{document} in this particle-hole pair approximation is69\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Xi _{-\infty }^\textrm{odd} = \sum _{N_d=0}^{(L-3)/2} O^{L}_{N_d} \text {e}^{\mu N_d} + L \text {e}^{\mu (L-1)/2} + L \text {e}^{ - |J| + \mu (L+1)/2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(L-1)/2$$\end{document} is the maximum number of non-overlapping pairs on a lattice of odd size L and the multiplicity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O^{L}_{N_d}$$\end{document} counts the number of distinct ways of putting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_d$$\end{document} pairs on a periodic lattice of odd size L. The last two terms are the defect contributions, an extra hole or particle not making up a particle-hole pair that costs an energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-J$$\end{document} for an extra particle and that leads to a multiplicity L counting the number of ways to place an extra hole or particle on a lattice made up of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(L-1)/2$$\end{document} pairs in sequence.

Since calculating \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O^{L}_{N_d}$$\end{document} is a non-trivial combinatorial problem, we follow a procedure similar to the one used in the even case and simplify the problem by considering two disjoint lattices of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(L+1)/2$$\end{document}. If we neglect mixed particle-hole pair microstates for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le N_d \le (L-3)/2$$\end{document}, i.e., those that have pairs on both lattices, we obtain the following approximate restricted particle-hole pair grand partition function70\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Xi _{d}^\textrm{odd} = \sum _{N_d=0}^{(L-3)/2} 2 C^{(L+1)/2}_{N_d} \text {e}^{\mu N_d} + L \text {e}^{\mu (L-1)/2} + L \text {e}^{ - |J| + \mu (L+1)/2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}which can be evaluated by completing the binomial sum and then subtracting the added terms:71\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Xi _{d}^\textrm{odd} = 2 \left( 1 + \text {e}^\mu \right) ^{(L+1)/2} - \text {e}^{\mu (L-1)/2} - \text {e}^{ \mu (L+1)/2} ( 2 - L \text {e}^{ - |J|} ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{(L+1)/2}_{N_d}$$\end{document} counts the number of distinct ways of placing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_d$$\end{document} particles on a lattice of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(L+1)/2$$\end{document}. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Xi _{d}^\textrm{odd}$$\end{document} correctly counts the multiplicity of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(L\pm 1)/2$$\end{document} states by construction. Although this approximation overcounts the one particle-hole pair state by 1 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L+1$$\end{document} instead of L) and overcounts the empty state by a factor of 2, we believe that it captures the essential physics of the strong anti-cooperativity case for L odd. The above approximation, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Xi _{d}^\textrm{odd}$$\end{document}, can be used to calculate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle _{d}^\textrm{odd} = (L \Xi _{d}^\textrm{odd})^{-1}\partial _\mu \Xi _{d}^\textrm{odd}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _{d}^\textrm{odd} = \sqrt{L^{-1} \partial _\mu \langle \varphi \rangle _{d}^\textrm{odd}}$$\end{document}, an approximate results that could then be compared with the exact ones. We will not, however, pursue this approach any further here.

Infinite range (IR) model

In what follows, we will use the infinite-range (IR) as a proxy for the more general long-range (LR) behavior. As detailed below, the IR results are qualitatively equivalent to those of the SR upon the mapping between the two models proposed in Eq. (73), which is exact to the lowest order in the coupling constant (dimensionless interaction potential). Similarly, the coupling constant in the LR, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_\textrm{LR}$$\end{document}, which would explicitly depend on the effective range of the interactions, would always be bound by the SR and IR couplings: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_\textrm{SR}< J_\textrm{LR} < J_\textrm{IR}$$\end{document}. Therefore, we can confidently state that we do not expect the LR case to show qualitatively different behavior aside from interpolating between the two other limiting cases.

The mapping between the SR and IR Lattice Gas can be established by performing a perturbative cumulant expansion of the grand partition function:72\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Xi= & \sum _{\left\{ \varphi _i\right\} } \text {e}^{-H_0-H_\textrm{int}} \nonumber \\= & \Xi _0 \langle \text {e}^{-H_\textrm{int}} \rangle _0 \nonumber \\\approx & \Xi _0 \text {e}^{-\langle H_\textrm{int}\rangle _0}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where the last line gives the first order cumulant expansion. The subscript 0 indicates a statistical average with respect to the non-interacting (Hill–Langmuir) model (ideal Lattice Gas). By calculating \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle H_\textrm{int}\rangle _0$$\end{document}, we obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle H_\textrm{int}\rangle _0 = -J_\textrm{SR} L \langle \varphi \rangle _0^2$$\end{document} for the SR model and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\frac{1}{2}J_\textrm{IR} L (L-1) \langle \varphi \rangle _0^2$$\end{document} for the IR model, results that suggest the following mapping between the two models:73\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J_\textrm{SR} \leftrightarrow \frac{1}{2} J_\textrm{IR}(L-1). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}This mapping is exact to lowest order in the coupling constant.

We adopt the following strategy to avoid carrying out an explicit calculation of the standard deviation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} directly within the cumulant approximation, which is cumbersome. The weak coupling results for the SR model can be found by expanding the exact parametric results for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle (\mu )$$\end{document} and the variance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v(\mu ) = \sigma ^2(\mu )$$\end{document} in powers of J. To first order in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_\textrm{SR}$$\end{document} we find74\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \frac{\sigma _0}{\sigma _\textrm{SR} }\right) ^2 = 1- 2 J_\textrm{SR} L \sigma _0^2 + \mathcal{O} (J_\textrm{SR} ^2) = 1- 2 J_\textrm{SR} \sigma _\infty ^2 + \mathcal{O} (J_\textrm{SR} ^2). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}We can then use the above mapping between the SR and LR models to get a result that is exact to first order in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_\textrm{IR}$$\end{document} for the IR model:75\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \frac{\sigma _0}{\sigma _\textrm{IR} }\right) ^2 = 1- L(L-1) J_\textrm{IR} \sigma _0^2 + \mathcal{O} (J_\textrm{IR}^2) = 1- (L-1) J_\textrm{IR} \sigma _\infty ^2 + \mathcal{O} (J_\textrm{IR}^2) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}One can check the above method for SR model at half-filling:76\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left[ 1- \left( \frac{\sigma _0}{\sigma _\textrm{SR} }\right) ^2 \right] = \frac{1}{2} J_\textrm{SR} + \mathcal{O} (J_\textrm{SR}^2 ) \qquad \text {(half-filling).} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}It would be useful to find a good interpolation formula for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} as an explicit function of L, J, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document} for both the SR and IR models. A reasonably good interpolation scheme can be set up by recognizing that the exact result for the standard deviation at half-filling for small J is to a very good approximation,77\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \frac{\sigma _0}{\sigma _\textrm{SR} }\right) ^2 = \exp \left( - 2 J_\textrm{SR} \sigma _\infty ^2\right) \times \left[ 1 + \mathcal{O} \left( \left[ \sigma _\infty ^2 J_\textrm{SR}\right] ^L\right) \right] \qquad \text {(half-filling)} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\infty ^2 = 1/4$$\end{document} at HF), which shows that it’s best to use an exponential resummation of the small J perturbation expansion.

Comparing the exact SR results with the above approximation shows that an even better approximation is to take the 5/2 root mean (a heuristic choice),78\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \frac{\sigma _0}{\sigma _{\textrm{SR}}}\right) ^2 \approx \left[ \exp \left( - 5 J_{\textrm{SR}} \sigma _\infty ^2\right) + L^{-5/2} \right] ^{2/5}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}to get a smooth interpolation. The above 5/2 root mean square average leads to our best approximation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\textrm{SR}$$\end{document}:79\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma _{\textrm{SR}} \approx \frac{\sigma _0}{\left[ \exp (- 5 J_\textrm{SR} \sigma _\infty ^2)+ L^{-5/2} \right] ^{1/5}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}This is a convenient form (see Fig. 20) because the dependence on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document} (through \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\infty$$\end{document}), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_\textrm{SR}$$\end{document}, and L is made explicit (since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _0=L^{-1/2} \sigma _\infty$$\end{document}). The crossover to the large J saturation limit is centered on80\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J_\textrm{co} \equiv \ln (L)/(2\sigma _\infty ^2). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_\textrm{co}$$\end{document} depends on L and filling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document}, increasing slowly with L and reaching a minimum at half-filling for fixed L. The convergence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} as a function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document} to the large J saturation limit is uniform only for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J < J_\textrm{co}$$\end{document}.

The approximation (79) is very accurate for all fillings (except possibly very close to zero and full-filling) as long as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{-5/2} \ll 1$$\end{document} (the case for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \approx 10$$\end{document}) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J < J_\textrm{co}$$\end{document} (see Fig. 20). For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{-5/2} \ll 1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J > J_\textrm{co}$$\end{document}, this approximation is accurate only within a window centered on half-filling. Because of the factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\infty ^2$$\end{document} in the argument of the exponential, convergence to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\infty$$\end{document} is fastest at HF and becomes slower and slower as moves away from HF to zero and full filling. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \approx 10$$\end{document}, the window of accuracy for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J<2$$\end{document} is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.2< \langle \varphi \rangle < 0.8$$\end{document}, which encompasses the experimental data window.

The interpretation of the above results is in accordance with our previous understanding: as long as the system is far enough from zero and full-filling, increasing J leads to a flattening of the Gaussian PDF with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} growing exponentially with J until the system reaches the crossover region, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J \approx J_\textrm{co}$$\end{document}, before saturating at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\infty$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J \gg J_\textrm{co}$$\end{document}. When the system reaches the crossover region the Gaussian form is no longer accurate because one (or two) of the wings starts to touch one (or two) of the boundaries, before morphing into a bimodal (two-state) system.

The corresponding result for the IR case can be obtained using the mapping (73). We note in passing that an approximate Gaussian effective free energy approach can be developed for the IR model along the lines of the one developed above for the non-interacting (Hill–Langmuir) model. This result can be used to find an extended Gaussian approximation for the IR model,81\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_IR(N; \langle \varphi \rangle ) \approx \frac{\exp \left[ -\frac{(\varphi - \langle \varphi \rangle )^2}{2 \sigma _\textrm{IR}^2} \right] }{{(2 \pi )^{1/2} \sigma _\textrm{IR} L}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where the variance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\textrm{IR}$$\end{document} can be approximated using the approximation for the SR model (79) and the mapping (73).

System size, fluctuations, and experimental precision

By proposing a pragmatic operational definition, we clarify here what we mean by a small system. Before doing so we note that if we were to attempt to qualify the size of an adsorption system by the ratio between the number of binding sites, L, and the size of a correlated domain (twice the correlation length), we would only be characterizing the Hill–Langmuir nature of the fluctuations, occurring when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2\xi /L \ll 1$$\end{document}, or the bimodal nature of the fluctuations, occurring when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2\xi /L \gg 1$$\end{document}, and not the feasibility of extracting the cooperativity from the experimentally measured relative occupancy fluctuations at equilibrium.

We propose here that a system be considered a small cooperative one if fluctuations, as quantified by the standard deviation of relative occupancy, are within experimental resolution and can be used to extract, using the theoretical framework that we have developed, the amplitude of the interaction strength, J, and that this strength be within the usual biophysical range, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le J \le J_\textrm{max} \approx 10$$\end{document}, for adsorption systems.

To quantify our capacity to resolve J theoretically, we therefore propose the following criterion: the derivative of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} with respect to J, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}'(J;L)$$\end{document}, must be larger than a given value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \sigma$$\end{document}, whose choice depends on the measurement precision, over a J range starting at 0. In what follows, for the application to the BFM, we shall choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \sigma = 0.02$$\end{document}, which is compatible with the experimental error for the data provided in Section “Application to the bacterial flagellar motor (BFM)”.

This choice is motivated by comparing the parametric plots in Fig. 3 to the experimental data (see Fig. 7). One can estimate the coupling J from the data when these fall in regions of the parametric plots where the lines corresponding to different values of J are spaced far enough apart compared to the experimental error. The highest resolution for positive values of J, and fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\ge 3$$\end{document}, is achieved at half-filling (HF), and for a value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^* \approx 2 \ln (L/2)$$\end{document} that maximizes the derivative of the standard deviation. These maxima correspond to the peaks of the curves in Fig. 11a where we plot \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}'(J;L)$$\end{document} as a function of J for different but fixed values of L and compare it with the threshold value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \sigma =0.02$$\end{document}.

We observe that for the choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \sigma =0.02$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^* \approx 39$$\end{document} is the maximum system size for which there is a feasibility window (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< J < 8$$\end{document}) starting at 0 for extracting the cooperativity from the experimental data. This threshold value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^*$$\end{document} can be recovered analytically by using the approximation Eq. (79) for the standard deviation, which is highly accurate near half-filling provided \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \gg 1$$\end{document}, to find \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}'(J=0; L) \approx 1/(8 L^{1/2})$$\end{document}. One can then solve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}'(J=0; L) = \Delta \sigma = 0.02$$\end{document} to obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^* = 1/(64 \Delta \sigma ^2)$$\end{document}. The same approximation leads to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}''(J=0; L) \approx 1/(32 L^{1/2})$$\end{document}. These approximations show that both the first and second derivatives of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}$$\end{document} at zero coupling (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=0$$\end{document}) decrease as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{-1/2}$$\end{document}. For high coupling, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J \gg J_\text {max}$$\end{document}, the system assumes a bimodal character and the standard deviation saturates at the infinite coupling limit.

We conclude, based on our definition and our choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \sigma$$\end{document}, that a system is small if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \le 39$$\end{document}. We also observe that for any system of finite size with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L > 39$$\end{document}, there is always a feasibility window for sufficiently strongly cooperative systems, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_{\textrm{min}}< J < J_\textrm{max}$$\end{document}, characterized by a peak at a value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J = J^*$$\end{document} that increases logarithmically with increasing L, but with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_{\textrm{min}} > 0$$\end{document}. Consequently, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \gg 39$$\end{document}, the standard deviation at HF is insensitive to J both at low coupling, where the system acts as a Hill–Langmuir one, and high coupling, where the system becomes bimodal.Fig. 11(a) Derivative of the standard deviation at half-filling, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}'(J;L) \equiv \text {d}\sigma _\text {HF}/\text {d}J$$\end{document}, plotted for systems of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=10,\, 39,\, 10^2,\,10^3,\,10^4$$\end{document}, respectively. The horizontal dashed gray line represents the threshold value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \sigma =0.02$$\end{document}. (b) Contour plot of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}'(J;L)$$\end{document} for positive values of J and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\ge 3$$\end{document}. The red contour lines correspond to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}'(J;L) = \Delta \sigma =0.02$$\end{document}.

(a) Derivative of the standard deviation at half-filling, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}'(J;L) \equiv \text {d}\sigma _\text {HF}/\text {d}J$$\end{document}, plotted for systems of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=10,\, 39,\, 10^2,\,10^3,\,10^4$$\end{document}, respectively. The horizontal dashed gray line represents the threshold value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \sigma =0.02$$\end{document}. (b) Contour plot of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}'(J;L)$$\end{document} for positive values of J and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\ge 3$$\end{document}. The red contour lines correspond to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}'(J;L) = \Delta \sigma =0.02$$\end{document}.

The (semi-log) contour plot in Fig. 11b gives the isolines of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}'(J)$$\end{document} as a function of the interaction potential J and the system size L, where the latter, for the sake of simplicity, is treated as a continuous variable. Recall the asymptotic behavior of the correlation length at half-filling for large values of the interaction potential38: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J \sim 2 \ln \left( 2 \xi _\text {HF}\right)$$\end{document}. Hence, if we were to plot the logarithm of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi _\text {HF}$$\end{document} instead of J on the horizontal axis, we would get a qualitatively equivalent contour diagram. The two red contour lines correspond to the constant value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \sigma =0.02$$\end{document}. The region between them indicates the set of parameters J and L for which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}'(J)>\Delta \sigma$$\end{document}. We finally conclude that a system of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=13$$\end{document}, like the one considered in the main text to describe the BFM, is well within the feasibility window for small systems.

Effective Hill coefficient

Following the analysis in11 on the logarithmic sensitivity of kinetics schemes, we can also quantify cooperative behavior by introducing an effective Hill coefficient which, for our system, takes the following form:82\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_\text {eff}= \frac{\text{ d }}{\text {d} \mu }\ln \left( \frac{\langle \varphi \rangle }{1-\langle \varphi \rangle }\right) = \frac{\sigma ^2 L}{\langle \varphi \rangle \left( 1-\langle \varphi \rangle \right) }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Here, we have exploited the relation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _\mu \langle \varphi \rangle = \sigma ^2 L$$\end{document}.

The effective Hill function defined above takes on a maximal value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_\text {eff}^*$$\end{document} at half-filling:83\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_\text {eff}^*(J) = 4\sigma _\text {HF}^2L, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}^2$$\end{document} is the square of the relative standard deviation at half-filling whose explicit dependency on J is captured by Eq. (5). This expression tends rapidly to zero for negative values of J (anticooperativity). Moreover, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=0$$\end{document}, the coefficient equals 1, as expected for the Hill–Langmuir case. Finally, by increasing the interaction potential, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {HF}$$\end{document} tends to 1/2, and so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^*_\text {eff}\rightarrow L$$\end{document}. This result is in agreement with the rule that at equilibrium, the Hill coefficient is expected not to exceed the maximal number of ligands that can bind simultaneously, which, in the case of an adsorption process, corresponds to the number of binding sites, L. Fig. 12 shows the sigmoidal allure of the Hill coefficient, bounded from above by the system size and below by 0.

It should be noted, however, that in11 it has been demonstrated that this upper bound for the effective Hill coefficient may be surpassed when the system is out-of-equilibrium. In such cases, the new limit is determined not by the size of the lattice, but by the number of states of the kinetic scheme whose exit rates increase with the concentration of the ligands.Fig. 12The effective Hill coefficient at half-filling from Eq. (83) as a function of the interaction potential J for a system of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=13$$\end{document}.

The effective Hill coefficient at half-filling from Eq. (83) as a function of the interaction potential J for a system of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=13$$\end{document}.

Note that Eq. (83) depends only on J, and not on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}. Therefore, by approximating \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_\text {eff}\approx H^*_\text {eff}$$\end{document} in Eq. (82) and solving for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^2$$\end{document}, we obtain an approximate, explicit analytic expression for the fluctuation amplitude as a function of the mean occupancy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document} and the interaction potential J:84\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma \approx \sqrt{\frac{H_\text {eff}^*}{L}} \sqrt{\langle \varphi \rangle (1 - \langle \varphi \rangle )} = 2 \sigma _{\text {HF}}(L,J) \sqrt{\langle \varphi \rangle (1 - \langle \varphi \rangle )}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}As shown in Fig. 13, this expression holds for non-negative J, and becomes exact at half-filling. It overestimates the fluctuations but performs well for intermediate occupancies. The discrepancy with respect to the exact curve for low and high values of the occupancy levels—increasing for intermediate values of J—vanishes in both limits \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J\rightarrow 0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J\rightarrow \infty .$$\end{document}Fig. 13Comparison between the exact result for the standard deviation (solid lines) and the approximate expression (dashed lines) that depends explicitly on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document}, J, and L [Eq. (84)].

Comparison between the exact result for the standard deviation (solid lines) and the approximate expression (dashed lines) that depends explicitly on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document}, J, and L [Eq. (84)].

Experimental data

The experimental methods used to probe the activity of single flagellar motors are described in detail in15,16. Briefly, E. coli cells lacking flagellar filaments and containing an endogenously biotinylated hook were adhered to a coverslip. The hook can bind to the surface of a straptividin-conjugated micron-sized bead. The slightly off-axis rotation of the bead in the liquid is tracked and provides a proxy for the angular rotation of the motor. The binding (unbinding) of individual stator units in the motor complex gives a sudden increase (decrease) in angular speed, enabling the quantification of the stator occupancy.

Each experimental trace15,16 was sampled at 10 Hz to avoid temporal correlations due to the hook of the BFM, which acts as a torsional elastic link between the motor and attached particle. For a given viscous load, the mean and standard deviation of the relative occupancy of stator units were calculated for each time point across all traces, yielding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi (t) \rangle$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma (t)$$\end{document}, respectively; the mean of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi (t) \rangle$$\end{document} over all time points gave \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \varphi \rangle$$\end{document}, and the mean of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma (t)$$\end{document} over all time points gave \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} (Fig. 7, Table 1). This approach was used because the experimental time traces were short, rendering them insufficient to assess the intracellular stationarity of single-cell traces. By calculating the mean and standard deviation in this manner, we assume that the intercellular variability is insignificant, which remains to be tested. Consequently, our method provides an upper estimate of the cooperativity J. Future studies probing individual motors for extended periods will enable a more accurate calculation of stator cooperativity.
